# Investigators

#--------------------
# Description_Notes_and_Keywords
#   Description: Ancient post oak trees from 200- to 400-years old dominate the ecosystem and eastern red cedar over 500-years old are also found on rocky fire-protected sites.  The ancient Cross Timbers are a crucial link in the oak archipelago that extends from Central America across the eastern United States and into southern Canada.  They provide critical wildlife habitat, particularly for neotropical migratory birds.  The old trees also record detailed climate and environmental information in the annual growth rings that extend centuries before settlement.  
#--------------------
# Publication
#   Authors: David W. Stahle
#   Published_Date_or_Year: 
#   Published_Title: The Ancient Cross Timbers
#   Journal_Name:
#   Volume:
#   Edition:
#   Issue:
#   Pages:
#   DOI:
#   Full_Citation: David W. Stahle. The Ancient Cross Timbers. University of Oklahoma Press (book), in press
#   Abstract: The Cross Timbers form the broad ecological transition zone between the forests of the eastern United States and the grasslands of the southern Great Plains.  Despite heavy clearing and other human disturbances, the Cross Timbers still preserve many areas of uncut old-growth forest, among the most numerous and pristine tracts of ancient forest left in the East.  The Cross Timbers located on steep rocky terrain were unsuitable for agriculture and the trees did not grow large enough nor fast enough to justify commercial lumber production.  As a result, thousands of acres of original ancient forest still remain in this crowded and rapidly developing region of the Southern Plains.  They are most often found along escarpments, ridgelines, and rocky slopes that constitute the western edge of the eastern deciduous forest from central Texas, across eastern Oklahoma, and into southeastern Kansas.
